#10637. How to Use Developer Time Tracking Data to Improve Sprint Planning and Estimates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint estimates usually fail because development teams heavily rely on memory and assumptions instead of evidence. They assume similar tasks will take the same amount of time. Based on these half-baked assumptions, most managers plan around ideal sprint working hours. The backlog then fills with commitments that look realistic on paper but often end up delayed.

Actual development rarely follows that plan. There are always unexpected bugs and task dependencies that consume more time than teams account for in their estimates. Even the buffer time set aside for unexpected work may not be enough. This causes tasks to take longer than expected and pushes sprint commitments behind schedule.

Developer time tracking helps teams make better estimates with historical data. It shows how long previous tasks actually took, which provides managers with an evidence-based reference when they are planning similar work.

Reviewing this data before committing to a sprint scope turns estimation from guesswork into an evidence-based process.

Historical time data offers deeper insights, depending on how you collect and analyze it. Most importantly, it explains why planned work took longer than expected.

Before sprint planning, the following records should be reviewed.

Completed task duration: Check the time spent on finished tasks. Managers should compare similar work to understand typical development effort.

Actual versus estimated hours: Management should observe and identify differences between planned and recorded hours. If there are repeated delays, to some extent, it indicates inaccurate estimates.

Carryover work: Review unfinished tasks from previous sprints. Determine whether poor estimates or external delays caused the spillover..

Interruptions: Measure time spent on urgent requests and production issues. These activities reduce the development capacity that you had planned earlier.

Meetings and ceremonies: Review the time being spent on planning sessions and sprint reviews.

Bug-fix time: Examine hours spent resolving defects. You can also compare it to the time for developing new features.

Blocked tasks: Managers should account for the tasks delayed by missing approvals or technical dependencies.

Capacity differences: Compare team availability across sprints. Include leave and staffing changes in that time as well.

These records provide the most value when reviewed together. For example, a task may finish three days late even if the development work stayed within its estimated hours. The actual issue could be an external dependency.

Teams should understand these differences before using historical data to estimate future work.

Historical records provide a useful starting point for estimating new tasks. However, teams must interpret the data rather than simply copy previous estimates.

The following approaches can improve estimation accuracy.

The first step is to consider completed tasks that resemble upcoming work. Managers should compare their requirements and technical complexity. Moreover, the development environment should be considered.

For example, suppose a team needs to estimate a new API integration. Three previous integrations required 12, 15, and 18 hours of active work.

The average is 15 hours. This gives developers a reference point for the new estimate.

However, the average doesn't tell the entire story. The new integration may involve unfamiliar authentication methods or more complicated testing.

Review the reasons behind differences in previous task durations. Check whether developers encountered unexpected bugs or unclear requirements.

As a project manager, I'd consider these details before estimating for the next project. In my experience, they provide more context than an average alone.

Inaccurate estimates can happen; it doesn’t mean that there is a definite planning problem. However, repeated overruns across similar tasks may reveal an underlying issue.

Research suggests the pattern is widespread. A review of software estimation surveys reports that 60–80% of software projects encounter effort overruns, with the average overrun falling between 30% and 40%.

Start by calculating the difference between estimated and actual effort. Here is the formula for it.

Estimation variance (%) = [(Actual hours − Estimated hours) ÷ Estimated hours] × 100

Suppose a task was estimated at 10 hours but needed 14 hours. The actual effort exceeded the estimate by 40%.

You can compare the results across similar tasks.

For example, database migrations may repeatedly require more time than expected. This means that the team might be overlooking data validation or additional testing. Once the cause is clear, adjust future estimates accordingly.

Avoid increasing every estimate by the same percentage. Different types of work involve different risks. Teams can also break large tasks into smaller parts. This makes hidden development activities easier to identify.

The aim is to correct recurring estimation errors without unnecessarily increasing the entire sprint backlog.

Task duration doesn't always reflect the hours spent developing a feature.

A study of developer workdays by Meyer and colleagues, which tracked 2,197 hours of activity across 20 developers, found that coding accounted for around 21% of the typical day. Collaborative work such as meetings and email took up roughly 24%.

Consider a developer who starts a feature on Monday and finishes it on Wednesday. The task took three days to complete. This is pretty straightforward, and the estimation could be based on it; however, the developer may have spent several hours in meetings. Other than that, urgent support requests could also have consumed additional time.

The actual development effort could be much lower than three full working days. This distinction matters during estimation.

Teams should record focused development time separately from interruptions. They should also track waiting periods caused by blocked tasks. A more appropriate approach would be to use active development hours to estimate similar future work. They should also account for recurring interruptions when calculating sprint capacity.

For example, a task may regularly wait for security approval. Adding more development hours to its estimate won't resolve that delay.

Instead, the team should recognize the dependency and consider its effect on delivery.

If managers are able to separate these activities, it will help create better estimates without confusing effort with elapsed time.

A precise estimate can create false confidence when a task involves substantial uncertainty.

Suppose similar tasks previously required between 12 and 20 hours. If you estimate the next task at exactly 14 hours, it will overlook important differences.

A range of 12–20 hours provides a clearer picture of the possible effort. Developers can then discuss what might push the task toward either end of the range.

For example, the lower estimate can be directly applied to integration, and the upper estimate might be considered for unfamiliar code or additional testing.

In such scenarios, if the requirements stay unclear, consider a short technical investigation before finalizing the estimate. This can help identify dependencies and reduce uncertainty.

However, estimate ranges should still support practical planning decisions. Teams need to determine how much capacity to reserve for the work.

Choose a planning assumption that reflects the task's risk and available capacity.

As developers learn more about the task, they can refine the estimate.

Historical time data should affect how much work a team accepts during sprint planning. Better estimates offer little value if teams continue making unrealistic commitments.

If several sprints end with unfinished tasks, it is best that you first review why the team missed its commitments in the first place. Once you have identified the causes, use those findings to reduce the next sprint's scope.

Teams should prioritize work that supports the sprint goal. Moreover, avoid adding extra tasks just because they appear manageable during planning.

Production incidents and urgent requests can disrupt planned development. To get more reliable estimations, review how much time these activities consumed in previous sprints. Then reserve an appropriate amount of capacity.

Historical estimates become less reliable in case unfamiliar technology or additional dependencies are involved. It is best to break complex tasks into smaller components where possible. This helps developers assess the work more accurately.

Nominal capacity represents scheduled working hours, whereas actual capacity reflects the time available after accounting for other commitments.

The Scrum Guide identifies previous performance and upcoming capacity as important inputs to sprint forecasting.

When you are selecting backlog items, consider both. Additionally, avoid committing every available hour or deducting the same interruption twice.

Historical time data is useful for sprint planning. Most development teams already use project management tools that include basic time tracking features.

However, these tools don't always break down how developers spend their working hours. For clearer time data, a feature-specific time tracker is more useful because it provides more details about tasks and workloads.

TimeBee is one such developer time tracking tool I've used. It tracks working hours and time spent on projects and tasks. It also provides attendance records and workforce activity reports.

With this data, teams can review how much time previous tasks required. For example, developers can record hours spent building a feature. They can also track debugging and revisions separately. Later, managers can compare these records with the original estimates. This helps them plan similar work more accurately.

TimeBee also tracks website and application activity. These reports can help teams understand how working hours are distributed.

However, not all activities reflect actual development effort. Sometimes a developer just spends time reviewing documentation or discussing technical problems. Such work may not always appear in TimeBee as active computer use.

Apart from that, attendance, leave, and overtime records from TimeBee can help managers calculate sprint capacity. They can review developer availability before deciding how much work to include in the next sprint.

One thing to remember is that developer time tracking should help teams understand workload patterns. It shouldn't be used to monitor every minute of a developer's day. Management should be careful that the aim is to improve estimates and make more realistic sprint commitments.

Sprint retrospectives should include a review of how the planning is going. If you have made a plan, it doesn’t mean you cannot make changes to it. During sprints, keep comparing original estimates with actual task hours. Track your unfinished work for recurring delays.

Ask developers what caused significant differences. Some tasks may have required additional testing. Others may have been delayed by dependencies. Select one specific improvement for the next sprint.

Not every estimation difference indicates a planning failure. Unexpected technical challenges are part of software development.

The objective is to improve forecasts over time rather than eliminate every variance.

Historical time data provides a more stable foundation for sprint planning. Developer time tracking helps teams understand what is actually happening within the sprint rather than creating unnecessary pressure for them.

Other than that, the data also reveals how interruptions affect available development hours.

However, time data should guide planning rather than dictate it.

1. What is the difference between sprint velocity and time-based capacity planning?

Sprint velocity measures how much work a team has completed during a sprint. It is usually measured in story points. Contrary to this, time-based capacity planning calculates the hours available for planned work. It accounts for meetings and other responsibilities.

Velocity helps predict future delivery based on past performance. Capacity planning shows whether the team has enough time to meet its commitments.

2. Which time tracking metrics are most useful for improving Agile sprint forecasts? The most useful metrics include estimated versus actual hours, task completion time, and unplanned work.

Bug-fix time, rework, and blocked tasks also provide valuable insights. Teams should also review available capacity across previous sprints.

These metrics help sort out recurring delays and estimation errors. They allow teams to forecast workloads more accurately and make realistic sprint commitments.

**3. How can development teams improve estimates when historical time tracking data is incomplete? **

Teams can start with available task records and completed sprint backlogs. Developer feedback can help fill information gaps. Similar tasks from previous projects may also provide useful estimates.

When data is limited, teams should use estimate ranges instead of fixed hours. Consistent time tracking in future sprints will gradually provide more reliable planning data.

4. How can time tracking data help balance workloads across developers during sprint planning?

Time tracking data shows how much time developers spend on assigned tasks and other responsibilities. Managers can review these records to identify uneven workloads. They can then distribute upcoming tasks based on available capacity.

Technical expertise and task complexity should also influence assignments. This helps prevent over commitment and supports a more balanced workload across the development team.

A group of elderly residents at a retirement home in France have been introduced GTA 6, and some are already hoping to get their hands on the game for Christmas.

The open-world crime game has attracted enormous attention worldwide, with fans taking time off work to play it. According to French broadcaster TF1, requests for annual leave in France increased by 8% around the game’s launch. Now, the game has been introduced to the older generations.

As reported by ICI Paris Île-de-France, the residents swapped their usual activities for an opportunity to explore the game’s virtual world, with many particularly enjoying its boat-driving sequences.

82-year-old Jacqueline was among the most enthusiastic participants, successfully collecting two packages of cocaine during a mission. “I had never touched a controller before 9am this morning. And by 2:30pm, I was fishing out two packages of drugs,” she joked.

Another resident, 86-year-old Lucienne, was equally impressed by the game’s visuals, describing them as incredible and comparing the experience to watching a real film.

Meanwhile, 75-year-old Yvan admitted that the controls were difficult to master, joking that he was no longer 20 and struggled to keep up with the action.

Staff explained that the residents had found driving and shooting particularly challenging, but enjoyed exploring the environment by boat.

The experience proved popular enough that Lucienne said she intended to ask her children for GTA 6 for Christmas, while Jacqueline suggested installing a console in the communal lounge.

#10634. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

Materials provided by PNAS Nexus. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10632. Brain-inspired chip runs near absolute zero and could transform quantum computing

Researchers at the University of Hong Kong (HKU) have unveiled a significant advance in cryogenic electronics that could help overcome key challenges in quantum computing and support future deep space missions. The team, from HKU's Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering and the Centre for Advanced Semiconductors and Integrated Circuits (CASIC), developed a programmable neuromorphic hardware platform capable of operating at temperatures near absolute zero.

The research was led by Professor Yuhao Zhang and PhD student Xin Yang. Their work introduces a new method for generating and controlling negative differential resistance (NDR) in industry standard Silicon Carbide (SiC) MOSFETs. Using this approach, the researchers demonstrated for the first time that a single transistor can reproduce the energy efficient "spiking" activity of biological neurons at temperatures as low as 10mK.

Brain-Inspired Hardware for Quantum Computing

Quantum computers depend on sophisticated control electronics to manage qubits, which are highly sensitive and must be kept at millikelvin temperatures. Existing silicon based control systems consume considerable power and produce unwanted heat, making it necessary to position them away from the qubits themselves. That distance creates extensive wiring requirements that can hinder performance and make large scale quantum computers more difficult to build.

"Our work introduces a hardware platform that can be integrated alongside quantum processors," said Professor Zhang. "By using the unique carrier dynamics in silicon carbide, we can create circuits that are thousands of times more energy-efficient than conventional electronics, significantly reducing the thermal load on cryogenic systems."

Silicon Carbide Reveals Unique Cryogenic Behavior

The team found that SiC MOSFETs display a strong "S-shape" NDR effect when cooled below 2K. This behavior is driven by electron-donor impact ionization (EDII). Unlike other technologies that depend on heat generated within a device, the newly observed mechanism arises directly from the material's atomic properties. As a result, it remains highly stable and can be reproduced consistently across different manufacturing batches.

"This is a robust and scalable approach," said Mr. Yang. "Because SiC is already used globally in electric vehicles and power grids, we can leverage existing industrial foundries to manufacture these cryogenic chips on 300-mm wafers."

From Artificial Neurons to Deep Space Missions

The study also demonstrated that these artificial neurons can be linked together, or "cascaded," into larger networks. This capability could enable advanced local data processing at cryogenic temperatures and improve important quantum computing functions such as quantum error correction and real time quantum control.

The potential applications extend well beyond quantum computing. Because the circuits are designed to operate reliably in extremely cold environments, they could also be valuable for deep space exploration. Future systems may be able to function in the harsh conditions found on the Moon's surface or in the distant regions of our solar system.

The findings were published in Nature Communications in a paper titled "Cryogenic neuromorphic circuits using gate-controlled negative differential resistance in silicon carbide."

Materials provided by The University of Hong Kong. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10629. How to Improve Email Deliverability Rates

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You wrote a good email. You hit send. Then half your list never saw it, because it went straight to spam.

That is a deliverability problem, and it costs more than most teams think. Every email that misses the inbox is a lead you paid for and never reached.

The good part is that deliverability is not luck. Mailbox providers like Gmail, Yahoo and Outlook judge you on a short list of things: who you are, who you send to, and how people react. Fix those and your inbox placement goes up.

This guide covers 10 email deliverability best practices in the order you should fix them, starting with SPF and ending with sender reputation.

Email deliverability is the share of your emails that land in the inbox instead of the spam folder or nowhere at all.

People often mix it up with delivery rate. They are not the same thing.

You can have a 99% delivery rate and still have most of your emails sitting in spam. That is why your email tool can say "delivered" while your open rate keeps dropping.

The big mailbox providers stopped treating authentication as optional.

Microsoft followed on May 5, 2025. If you send more than 5,000 emails a day to Outlook.com, Hotmail or Live addresses, you need all three records in place or your mail goes to junk.

Send less than 5,000 a day? Follow the rules anyway. The same checks decide inbox placement for small senders too, just without the public warning.

SPF (Sender Policy Framework) is a TXT record in your DNS. It lists the servers allowed to send email from your domain.

When your email arrives, the receiving server checks that list. If the sending server is not on it, the email looks suspicious.

DKIM (DomainKeys Identified Mail) adds a digital signature to each email. The receiving server uses a public key in your DNS to check two things: the email came from you, and nobody changed it on the way.

Your email provider gives you the DKIM record. You paste it into your DNS and switch signing on.

DKIM also survives forwarding, which SPF does not. If you can only get one of the two perfect, get DKIM right.

DMARC ties SPF and DKIM together. It tells mailbox providers what to do when an email fails both checks, and it sends you reports on who is sending mail under your domain name.

p=none means "take no action, just send me reports." It is the minimum Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft accept from bulk senders.

Don't stop there. Read the reports for a few weeks, fix any real sender that is failing, then move up:

One more thing: DMARC needs alignment. The domain in your From address has to match the domain used in SPF or DKIM. Plenty of senders have all three records and still fail DMARC because their email tool signs with its own domain.

If you'd rather not handle this in-house, you can also hire a B2B cold email marketing agency such as IntentSignal, Martal or Belkins. A good agency will set up the domains, mailboxes, warm-up and SPF, DKIM and DMARC records for you, and keep an eye on deliverability while the campaigns run.

A new domain has no history. If it sends 10,000 emails on day one, it looks exactly like a spammer who just bought a domain.

Warm-up means starting small and growing a little each day, so mailbox providers can watch how people react to you.

Send to your most active contacts first. Their opens and replies are what build trust early. If bounces or complaints jump, hold at that volume until they settle.

And if you run cold outreach, do it from a separate domain or subdomain. That way a bad campaign can't hurt the domain your invoices and password resets come from.

A bigger list is not a better list. Dead addresses and people who stopped caring pull your numbers down.

Never buy a list. Bought lists are full of spam traps, which are addresses that exist only to catch senders who email people without permission. Hitting a few of them can get your domain blocklisted.

Yes, you lose some sign-ups. What you keep is real people with working inboxes who actually want your emails. That means fewer bounces, fewer complaints and better opens from day one.

It also keeps bots and fake addresses out of your forms, which matters a lot if your sign-up form is public.

Most email platforms add these for you. Check that yours does.

These two numbers tell you the most about where you stand.

The complaint figures come from Gmail's own rules. The bounce figures are the working limits most email platforms use.

A 0.3% complaint rate is only 3 complaints per 1,000 emails. It does not take much to cross that line.

If complaints are climbing, ask three questions:

Most complaint problems come from one of those three, not from the email itself.

Spam filters got smarter. A single word like "free" won't sink you anymore. Patterns will.

Replies help a lot. When someone replies, the mailbox provider sees a real conversation. Ask a question in your welcome email and you'll get some.

Sender reputation is the score mailbox providers give your domain and IP. Everything above feeds into it. A good score means inbox. A bad one means spam, no matter how good the email is.

You can't see the exact score, but these free tools get you close:

Check them weekly, and right after any big campaign.

A few habits protect your reputation over time:

Aim for 95% or higher inbox placement. Below 90% means something needs fixing, usually authentication or list quality.

The common causes are missing or broken SPF, DKIM or DMARC records, a high complaint rate, an old list with lots of dead addresses, or a new domain that sent too much too soon. Check authentication first because it is the fastest to fix.

DNS fixes like SPF and DKIM take effect within a day or two. Reputation is slower. Expect two to six weeks of steady, clean sending before inbox placement recovers.

Yes. The 5,000 mark is where Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft start enforcing openly. Small senders are still judged on the same signals, and Gmail asks every sender for at least SPF or DKIM.

Only if you send enough to keep it warm, roughly 100,000 emails a month or more. Below that, a good shared IP from a trusted provider usually performs better.

Sender reputation covers both your IP address and your domain. Domain reputation is the part tied to your domain name, and it follows you even if you switch email providers. Gmail leans on domain reputation more than IP.

If you only have an hour this week, spend it on SPF, DKIM and DMARC. Nothing else works until those pass.

After that, clean your list and keep an eye on complaints. Send emails people asked for, to people who still want them, from a domain that proves who it is. Do that for a few weeks and your emails will land in the inbox again.

#10627. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

#10624. What we've been playing - "Whether he means to or not, he serves sass all over town"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Another week has climaxed and I'm so sorry you had to read that on a Saturday morning. But here we are again, with our regular feature, where we talk about what we've been playing in order that you do too. This week, Marie has been staring at monster butts; Connor has capitulated to World of Warcraft; Kelsey is discovering the joys of a beloved Zelda game for the first time; and Bertie enjoys someone being punched in the face, because I guess he's that sort of person?

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. And now onward!

My favourite thing so far about Ocarina of Time is the judgement plastered on little Link's face when he encounters, well, almost everyone. Whether he means to or not, he serves sass all over town, and in Kakariko Village, and I am very much here for it.

Also, I wasn't prepared to come face-to-face with a monster's butt cheeks. At all. Imagine the scene: I'm enjoying the tunes of the Lost Woods, processing the changes that have happened to Hyrule in the last seven years, all while making my way to the Forest Temple. Then the Moblin's turn their backs to reveal... Well, everything.

This week I've spent an ungodly amount of time playing World of Warcraft: Forever (you may have read my preview on the website) and have been essentially glued to it during every minute of free time I've had away from work. I've got both my main professions above the level 225 mark and I'm firmly in the thick of it now. Levels take longer, items are actually getting good, and I've had to make a second alt character for extra bank space.

As a result, I've not been to the gym in a while. I actually got one of those emails asking why I've fallen off, offering some tips to get back into the routine. Sorry Nuffield Health, my sleep schedule is ruined and I've substituted proper workouts for squats and basic at-home cardio. Surely, this can't go wrong...

I don't have the same level of nostalgia for Ocarina of Time that my peers do, but that has not stopped me from enjoying every minute of running around Hyrule smashing pots and trying not to cry over the touching cutscenes and, well, Saria.

The game feels like Dark Souls for children (endearing) and that is completely my jam. It's magical, satisfying, and often sad. There's creepy enemies as well as cute ones, trap floors and doors, mysterious NPCs... and my heart just feels so warm whenever I'm playing it. My fingers are now crossed that there's a Majora's Mask remake in the pipeline over at Nintendo. (As well as a Super Mario 64 remake, which I admit, is pushing it. But for what it’s worth, I'd probably sell my soul to see it get the Ocarina treatment.)

It takes me a while to settle with games; it takes me a while to settle full stop. I suppose in Fire Emblem's case I'm out of touch, seeing as the last instalment I played was on 3DS. But I struggled with the garish enthusiasm early on and the whole thing seemed overly simple. Nevertheless, I've settled a bit now, and now that the game's layering on more strategy and Intelligent System's expertise is coming to the fore, I'm beginning to enjoy myself much more.

I wasn't expecting it to be quite so boobsy, though. Was Fire Emblem always this way? Perhaps it's my fault for picking the scantily clad queen Theodora, though that sort of presentation isn't reserved only for her. She's no wallflower, though. My favourite moment so far was when she got off her elephant during a parade - yes, her elephant - and walked over to punch a teenage boy in the face for halting proceedings. Apparently she was doing him a favour.

#10623. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.

Materials provided by University of Barcelona. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10620. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

Five Ways Quantum Mechanics Affects Everyday Life

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Original written by Lesley Henton. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10619. Vice City was the GTA that established Rockstar's killer formula - and 24 years on, it's still one of the best games ever made

"Get the limey out of here," Burt Reynolds tells the producers of GTA: Vice City during a recording session for the game, as Rockstar co-founder Dan Houser enters the studio. "I'm not going to work if this limey Brit is in here!" Houser refuses to leave, so the story goes - he just came down to meet one of his heroes! - and he and Reynolds end up chest-to-chest, about to come to blows, before a studio engineer breaks up the fight.

I can't verify the truth of this story. It's one of those development rumours that's been around for a long time, originally written in a book about how games conquered pop culture called "All Your Base Are Belong to Us" by Harold Goldberg. But I like to believe it's true, because in this one moment, the two crucial strands of GTA: Vice City's DNA helix together: its star power and Rockstar's arrogance.

And I'm not saying that in a pejorative way at all. I think a game of Vice City's scope - stretched to breaking with ambition, budget, and technical wizardry - couldn't exist without the ego trip of Rockstar at its highest. This is a game that (somehow!) started as a bit of DLC for GTA 3, supposedly intended to launch as a mission pack when Rockstar North figured out how to port it to PC. GTA 3 launched in October 2001, and by some miracle - and lots of crunch, no doubt - Vice City hit shelves in October 2002.

This game - a game that would come to represent everything the GTA series is, for generations to come - was put together in about a year. I'd feel pretty full of myself, too, if I was able to crank out that sort of quality in that sort of timeframe. With a development budget of approximately $5 million (massive at the time, even though it seems paltry against GTA 6's rumoured $1 billion+) and a marketing spend of $13.2 million, Vice City's budget was indicative of a series shifting toward becoming a cultural milestone - a standard bearer for video games as a whole, and as explosive and attention-grabbing as Hollywood's biggest events.

It's not like Rockstar needed to borrow credibility from the movie industry to inject some panache into its work, but even so, I think it's in its vortex of unbelievable Hollywood talent that Vice City really makes its mark. This was the first time Rockstar used a voiced protagonist, and by the developer's own admission, it was "a riddle" they had to solve. It didn't come easy; by making the protagonist a fullyfleshed-out character, players also had to like him - not an easy ask when he's a bonafide psychopath with a fetish for revenge like Tommy Vercetti. But Ray Liotta pulls it off without breaking a sweat. The actor has gone on record saying it was a rough gig ("You're creating a character that's not there before", he bizarrely complained in one interview), and Vice City director Navid Khonsari often moaned about the actor's unpredictable moods, so I imagine the mood in the booth wasn't great, but the result? The result is phenomenal.

You shouldn't like Vercetti. He's arrogant, he's brash, he's a totem for all the worst elements of misogynistic 80s excess. And yet, because he's such a cartoon caricature of a Miami gangster, he becomes a sort of quasi-romantic figure; a mob stooge who's been set up by his old 'business associates' and by the very fabric of the American Dream. Vice City is, in a sense, a redemption story dressed in the clothes of a revenge one. Vercetti dreams of being a self-made man, of taming this new frontier of unbridled capitalism and clawing back the years that were taken from him by the mob in Liberty City.

From the moment he arrives in town and a cocaine deal quickly goes south, Vercetti is on the back foot. In the opening hours of the game, he - and, of course, you by extension - work for pocket change, picking up menial jobs here and there while meeting all the freaks and strangers Vice City has to offer. There are no less than 50 story characters here, brought to life by Ray Liotta, Tom Sizemore, Luis Guzmán, Gary Busey, Danny Trejo, Debbie Harry, Danny Dyer, Philip Michael Thomas, William Fichtner, and many, many more besides.

Playing Vice City again in 2026, I was absolutely floored by this cast - and how well their personalities and mannerisms translate to the almost puppet-like visuals Rockstar deployed for the game. Reynolds - despite not having a good time in the booth, if the story up top is anything to go by - is a highlight; the bombast and bravado of his Avery Carrington feels like a prototype for some of the more extreme characters we meet later in the series. I think I can even see a bit of Trevor in him, you know? It's how the Housers would take a trope - say, ruthless Texan oil magnate - and amplify it to its furthest, most cartoon extreme: what does this person look like with all the guardrails off, with the heady intoxication of unlimited capital at their disposal? For Trevor, that trope is 'unhinged redneck' and the cartoonish extreme is 'without a single shred of a conscience'. You can apply the formula to just about anything in the series, and it makes sense.

For Tommy Vercetti, though, it's slightly more nuanced. He isn't just 'Scarface' with a touch more humanity - no, he's a tragic figure, betrayed by all his closest friends. In striving to take back some power and gain a little more control over his life, he ends up decapitating the local drug smuggling scene and taking over as its head. The first half of Vice City is Sisyphean; an endless, uphill slog to scrabble just enough money to get by. Then, all of a sudden, the world opens up: you're now head of a drug smuggling ring, operating out of a suspiciously Scarface-like mansion, with the city at your fingertips.

The way the game, previously tightly-coiled and a bit miserly with its freedom, now unfurls in front of you is a perfect reflection of this new-found power. Suddenly, your world isn't governed by the little mission icons on your minimap, you're not being told what to do over the phone by contacts wanting something from you. No, you're calling the shots. This is the 80s, so those that are making it big are making it big, and those that are failing are failing. In a breathless display of uber-capitalist Yuppie thinking, you can even buy out nearly-bankrupt businesses and run them to your liking. The strategy is two-fold: one, you gain fronts for your other illicit activities, and two, you diversify your income streams and safeguard your assets should any one element fail.

And it's here, in its glorious second half, that Vice City becomes a breezy, beautiful bit of open-world catnip that fuses gameplay and story in a way few games have done so successfully since. You can buy a strip club (where you can launder cash), the Malibu Club (which comes with an outlandish set of missions culminating in a heist), the Cherry Popper ice cream van company (a front for dealing), the boatyard (a drug smuggling base run by two excellent potheads), Sunshine Autos (a fence for stolen cars), Kaufman Cabs (headed up by an old controller played by Debbie Harry), Interglobal Studios (an adult movie production company), and the Print Works (a counterfeiting operation). The roleplaying as Vercetti takes off; you're a bigshot touring his empire, helping out here and there, meeting your crew, setting up deals. This should feel like drudgery; workaday tedium unbecoming for a legitimate businessman like yourself. But every job, every bit of flavour text and dialogue, feeds into your growing sense of power… and Vercetti's unstoppable ego.

Alongside these key story beats are Vice City's non-essential tasks - which feel like a precursor to the 'Strangers and Freaks' missions in later GTA and Red Dead games - including the Love Fist missions. These, I think, are the best missions in the game (I'd also like to note here that 'Jez Torrent' is an amazing name for a New Wave of British Heavy metal frontman). But it's the way these core and non-essential missions dovetail that feels so great, and where the open world of Vice City really comes into its own.

In many ways, Rockstar's work here created the blueprint for what would become the Grand Theft Auto 'formula'; galvanising cinematic setpieces with free-roam activities and meta-progression that manage to alloy player agency with this sense of perpetual, unstoppable momentum. And it's all fun, too. There are some abysmal tasks, sure - piloting RC aircraft to ruin building sites, navigating boats with the wonkiest physics you've ever seen around stunt courses, waiting 15 minutes a pop for rare cars to spawn - but generally speaking, Vice City has the fun factor.

I don't think that's something I can say of other GTA games: I think often, they're a bit bloated around the middle, padded out with baggy missions and feckless busywork to give the illusion of depth and breadth. GTA 4 is also one of the most dour and oppressive open worlds I've experienced (this is a compliment, mostly), but it's not particularly fun. GTA 5, especially the Trevor segments out in the rural counties, can get a bit one-note - why introduce us to this massive world, to the state of Los Santos, if you're going to quarantine us to the rural outskirts when we're just starting to familiarise ourselves with the city? Why put up rails in a world begging us to revel in its open-ended sandbox? The only time that happens in Vice City is at the start, and the game is better for it: 'if you want to live the Scarface fantasy, buddy, you've got to earn it'.

And there's so much to love about the vibes of Vice City. The guys in shorts smoking pot on the beach; the girls in bikinis rollerblading down the promenade; the neon-drenched slick of a Florida night in the rain; the still-unbelievable who's who of music, deployed with panache in certain cutscenes; the incidental chatter of coked-up presenters on the radio; the distinct realisation of the Haitian and Cuban districts on the map; how 'downtown' actually feels like downtown. You'd think all this would have aged out of relevance by now - especially with GTA 5 being as lush and premium as it is - so it's a real credit to all those Rockstar employees that no doubt spent countless nights sleeping under their desks that Vice City still feels this good now, in 2026.

GTA: Vice City is still one of the best games ever made, I think, and probably one of the most important, too. Buoyed by Rockstar's ambition, and propelled to mainstream success by the strength of its cast, it represented video games coming out of their awkward teenage years and blossoming into young adults; biting off slightly more than they could chew, but cocksure and confident all at the same time.

Given how much of Vice City is built around the visibility and power of its cast, it's disappointing we're never going to see these characters really represented in any other GTA title again. Liotta, already unhappy about the process of recording his lines, reportedly refused to come back as Vercetti for San Andreas. And that's a real shame because - by former lead writer Dan Houser's own admission - Liotta saved his "awful writing" with his talent. The actor passed away in May 2022.

Though he balked with Reynolds, Vice City director Khonsari could usually corral the rampant ego of his Hollywood hires in the GTA studio. "If you want me to break it down to you," he’d say, "these games gross over half a billion dollars, more than all of your movies put together!" I think you can see that bravado in the spirit of Vice City, and reflected in the runaway ego of Tommy Vercetti. And I think - without that almost toxic sense of self-belief - Vice City would never have happened.

Grand Theft Auto: Vice City 10th Anniversary Edition

#10617. GTA 6 developer pays touching tribute to late mother by immortalizing her in game

A GTA 6 developer has paid tribute to his late mother by including a memorial to her in the finished game.

After more than a decade of anticipation, Grand Theft Auto 6 finally launched on November 19, 2026, bringing players back to Vice City and the wider fictional state of Leonida.

Rockstar’s latest open-world game is packed with intricate environmental details, hidden references, and Easter eggs, with players continuing to uncover secrets scattered throughout its sprawling map. A developer who worked on the game has shared a tribute to his late mother that players can find.

In a heartfelt Instagram post, GTA 6 developer Matt Green revealed that his late mother has been honoured with a special memorial just north of the in-game region, Hamlet.

Green’s mother, Bev, passed away approximately eight months before the game’s release, meaning she never had the opportunity to see the project her son had been working on.

However, the developer was able to ensure that his mother would remain part of the game through a permanent tribute within its fictional world.

“I’m incredibly grateful that she’s able to be honoured inside the world of VI – at a special memorial just north of Hamlet,” they said. “Miss you Mum.”

A post shared by Matt Green (@mattxgreen)

The developer recalled begging his mother to buy him a copy of GTA Vice City as a child. Despite her initial reluctance, she eventually agreed, a decision he believes ultimately set him on the path to working at Rockstar Games.

“She was super proud and excited to see what I’d been working on but unfortunately I never got to show her,” they wrote. “She’s been gone almost 8 months now and sometimes it still doesn’t feel real.”

#10615. What's the best GTA?

As we teeter on the brink of gaming's biggest launch of 2026 - perhaps even decade - so many questions swirl: which GTA 6 cars will make the best vrrrrm? Is it possible for me to be any more obsessed with that Justice theme song? On a scale of one to ten, how many dongs? Answers will inevitably come, but for now - as we crank up the Phil Collins and roar deeper into Eurogamer's GTA Week - there's only one question we're concerned with. Yes, it's the return of our irregular series The Big Question, in which we ponder another topical talking point before wafting it lovingly over to you. And this time around we're asking, what's the best GTA?

Because I'm old, my journey with the series began all the way back in 1997, with the advent of GTA 1. I still vividly remember sitting with the included paper map sprawled across my lap, wide-eyed at the top-down sandbox as my hands reckoned with the weird controls. I remember feeling dizzy at the thrill of high-speed freedom (and possibly the antiquated frame rate) as I tore across Liberty City, San Andreas, and Vice City - particularly whenever this catchy little number came on. I was never massively into those early GTAs, though, not even when the series shifted to full 3D with GTA 3. And it wasn't until Vice City's arrival in 2002, that I began to properly appreciate the series' rhythms - even though I'll readily admit I was at my happiest tearing through the streets with the radio blaring. Vice City's soundtrack, from A Flock of Seagulls to Loverboy and everything in between, is still unmatched today.

But is it the best GTA? Vice City is certainly the GTA I've fondest memories of, even if I did mostly play it as a glorified jukebox. And as much as I've a soft spot for Chinatown Wars on Nintendo DS (a game whose propulsive style and classic top-down aesthetic did a fine job of melding GTAs old and new), Vice City is the GTA I've returned to most. But what about others on the Eurogamer team? I asked, they've answered with their own personal bests, and when they're done yammering, it's over to you - there's even a poll! And don't forget we'll be exploring the series more thoroughly as GTA Week continues.

I think, even to this day, GTA: Vice City represents what I think the best Grand Theft Auto is. The vibes, even 24 years later, are immaculate: its vision of Miami in the '80's, with all its cocaine-fueled, sun-washed Yuppie excess makes for the perfect backdrop in a game that's all about freedom and fantasy. The first half of Vice City orientates you wonderfully - casting you as Ray Liotta's arrogant, low-key psychopathic Tommy Vercetti - and letting you quietly get to know the rules of this frontier of narcotic hedonism.

Then you're let loose, wide-eyed with nostrils flared, into the second half of the game: a completely open-ended experience that's all about money, moxie, and madness. Take over businesses, build the Vercetti empire, strongarm your way into Vice City's seedy underbelly in any way you see fit. Even in a more nascent form, Rockstar managed to combine open world 'side activities' with its main narrative, building on everything GTA 3 put down without losing sight of the core theme (something I think San Andreas is guilty of; that game goes off the rails by the third island, whereas I think Vice City always keeps its focus quite neat).

With Vice City, it feels like Rockstar was at the peak of its power, the excesses of its neon-soaked in-game world reflected in the developer's ambitions in the real one: Ray Liotta, Burt Reynolds, Tom Sizemore, Luis Guzmán, Gary Busey, Danny Trejo, Debbie Harry, Danny Dyer, Philip Michael Thomas, William Fichtner… that is an absolutely ridiculous cast, and - in researching the game for something else I'm writing - I have learned there was so much on-set tension between this uncontrollable star power and Rockstar's development team that Reynolds nearly had a fistfight with studio co-founder Dan Houser.

That tension, and that slightly arrogant ambition on behalf of Rockstar, manifest in the bones of Vice City. It's a game struggling against its confines, made by a developer feeling like there is nothing in the world that can stop them. I imagine GTA 6 may feel the same, so how fitting, then, that we're going back to Vice City for the pleasure of it.

Barring the original, Vice City was the GTA I played longest, so those are the cornerstones of my GTA experiences. But San Andreas left the biggest impression on me. And I think it's because it was the first game I played that really attempted to portray Black culture, albeit through a stereotyped and potentially problematic West Coast rap lens' - I don't know how well it holds up today.

I was very into West Coast rap and hip-hop at the time, and the idea I could partially experience the lives which were referenced in that music so frequently - that I could live a kind of NWA origin story through the character CJ - really appealed to me, as it obviously did to many other people. It felt like I was coming straight outta Compton, as it were. It was rap tourism, which was probably totally fake and insincere to the actual experience of a life like that, but I drank it up all the same.

Fast-forward to GTA 5, which I think of as San Andreas mark two (in a similar way that I think of GTA 6 as Vice City mark two), and it's still startlingly apparent upon playing it - even now, many years after it was first released - that no other company attempts Black culture in the same way. No one even dares. And while I'm totally not the right person to evaluate how well Rockstar carries it off, it does make the GTA experience unique.

Back to San Andreas and I also enjoyed its ridiculous bloat: the gym sessions, the mini-games, eating burgers. It was like living a life in that sunny city, a life I couldn't live otherwise. I'm excited that some of those features are making their way back into GTA 6.

I've never visited America, but I've never been more fond of any city in the world than New York. It may not have been called that in GTA 4, but it is the closest any game has got to making me realise my dream of living in and exploring the city.

GTA 4's Liberty City has one of my favourite aesthetics in games: the dreary, autumnal look where the most sunshine you get is an overcast day. Perhaps New York's relatively compact layout (compared to sprawling Los Angeles) is what made it possible to replicate most of its boroughs in-game, and what helped it achieve a sense of place no other GTA comes close to. I knew my way around so much of that map just by landmarks. I was surprised how much of the area around Niko's house I remember when I revisited the game five-ish years ago.

I also consider GTA 4 to be the last entry in the series' classic era. Despite being the first to use Rockstar's now-standard RAGE engine, its controls were just about as loose and arcadey as its 3D predecessors. RAGE's weighty animations gave its characters and cars the look and motion of their real-life counterparts without interfering too much with how they felt to control. GTA 4 stood on the boundary of old and new while delivering the series' next evolution - a tough act for any sequel to follow.

So that's The Big Question, and the best - or at least our favourite - GTAs... now it's over to you!

N64, Nintendo Game Boy, PC, PS1, PS3, PSP
