#10614. How to Use IP Geolocation to Stop Bots and Fraud (Without Hurting UX)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Several methods can help you deal with online threats and strengthen security. All are effective, but each has a trade-off. For example, the harder you make it for bots to affect your website’s performance, the more likely you are to frustrate real customers.

Those of you who browse online daily know that credential stuffing, fake signups & payment fraud keep growing. To prevent fraud, businesses install CAPTCHAs & hard blocks that treat bots and legitimate users the same, diverting them to your competitors.

IP geolocation maps an IP address to a physical location & network details, letting you spot suspicious traffic before it reaches sensitive actions. Used well, it’s an invisible, low-friction risk signal. In this guide, you will learn how to flag high-risk activity and apply soft geo-fencing with step-up authentication.

Today, an IP lookup returns far more than a country & city. It can also reveal several other parameters, like

Each of these fields acts as a clue telling who is really behind a request.

This data powers anomaly detection. You can start by building a baseline for normal behavior for each user with aspects like

Then, you can watch for deviations from that pattern. An attacker won’t look like the account’s real owner, so you'll catch them.

Geolocation makes several common attack patterns easy to spot:

The first is traffic bursts from data center ASNs. These are bots, as real customers don’t browse from cloud servers

Many accounts created from one IP or subnet. This is a clear sign of fake signups

Impossible travel, such as logins from two distant locations within minutes

Timezone vs. IP mismatch. A device’s clock disagrees with its apparent location.

Note: Geolocation is just a signal, not a verdict. VPNs, travel, & mobile carriers can all create false alarms, so it works best when combined with other signals. That’s exactly why a proportional, soft response beats a hard block.

In short, IP geolocation turns a bare IP address into context, helping you separate real users from automated or fraudulent traffic.

Not every IP geolocation anomaly deserves the same response. The simplest way to stay consistent is to define a clear risk-signal framework.

That can be a short list of checks. Each check can have a weight that reflects how strongly it suggests fraud.

With risk scoring, you can turn these checks into a decision. You just have to assign each signal a numeric weight.

For example, medium can be 10 & 30 can be high.

Now, you need to add up every signal that comes up & map the total to thresholds like

A single medium signal passes quietly, but several can trigger action.

If you treat this as an automatic block, it would punish legitimate customers. Instead, let it raise the score & you take action only if it stacks with other signals, like a hosting IP or a new device.

Now that you know how to quantify the risk, here is how you can protect yourself without damaging UX.

Before soft, let us learn about hard blocking. This is where you completely deny or permanently ban traffic from specific geographic regions.

Now, hard blocking by country may look effective, but it can backfire quickly. Here is how.

In the worst case, determined bot operators simply rotate to IPs in allowed regions, so the only people you stop are your real customers.

This is where the balance between security and user experience becomes critical. As the digital strategist and Founder at LawRank, Mariano Rodriguez emphasizes, "Your website's security should be a silent guardian, not a roadblock. If your fraud prevention creates friction, you aren't just stopping bots—you're turning away real clients. True digital trust is built when backend protections are seamless and invisible to the legitimate user."

Now, with soft geo-fencing, you can respond to risk proportionally instead of making a simple allow-or-deny call. Instead of totally banning the users, you adjust the level of friction to match the risk score from the previous section:

**Note: **For soft geo-fencing, passkeys are safest because they are phishing-resistant. Only a legitimate user can take the biometric tap and pass.

Before we start, let’s understand Express.js middleware. An Express.js middleware for Geolocation Risk Scores is a custom or 3rd party function that intercepts the incoming HTTP requests, extracts the client's IP address, fetches a security risk profile based on their geographic location & attaches that data to the request object (req) before it reaches your route handlers.

Here is how you can use it for fraud prevention and bot mitigation:

Note: You can make it stronger by adding velocity checks (too many attempts from one IP or account) & device fingerprints.

From a broader angle, IP geolocation isn't the final verdict. It is just a signal. But with this signal, you can score each request, respond in proportion to the risk & save hard blocks for clear abuse. This increases the friction for bots and keeps the flow easy for legitimate users.

#10613. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."

Materials provided by University of Illinois Grainger College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10612. Clash Royale recruits creator of AI cheating tool to help catch cheaters

Clash Royale developer Supercell has recruited the creator of an advanced AI cheating tool to help crack down on cheating in the game.

AI cheating has become a growing controversy in Clash Royale, with increasingly sophisticated bots reportedly capable of outperforming skilled human players. Players highlighted claims that an AI-controlled account named ‘Clasher’ had reached the top of the game’s global leaderboard.

The situation prompted calls for Supercell to intervene, with the developer addressing the controversy on November 20. In a statement posted on X, Clash Royale warned that using AI or other tools to cheat was a “royal crime,” declaring that offending players would be banished from the Arena.

On November 21, the official Clash Royale X account announced that it had caught the creator of an AI cheating tool and recruited them to help develop new anti-cheat technology.

“The creator of the AI cheating tool has been caught,” Supercell announced in a statement framed as a royal decree from the King. “He’s agreed, entirely of his own free will, to join forces with the Clash Royale team.

“And who better to help us catch cheaters than the person who built the most advanced cheating tool we’ve seen in the Arena? Together, we’re building new technology to outlaw this behaviour,” the statement concluded.

An official update by royal decree of the King.The creator of the AI cheating tool has been caught.He’s agreed, entirely of his own free will, to join forces with the Clash Royale team. And who better to help us catch cheaters than the person who built the most advanced…

Supercell has not publicly identified the individual nor revealed if they were behind the ‘Clasher’ account. Supercell’s existing fair-play measures already combine automated detection with human moderation, including regular checks of leaderboards and competitive players.

The newly announced collaboration suggests the company is looking to strengthen those systems against increasingly sophisticated AI cheating tools.

#10610. Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas is GTA 5 without the finesse - so why is the 2004 classic such a strange delight to return to?

There is a place in Whitewood Estates where a promising stretch of blacktop suddenly gives way to the desert. It feels like the road has been erased, a sentence left unfinished. It's quietly strange, a weird bit of urban wrongfooting. But I guess it makes some kind of sense. California, after all, is home to California City, a town that was planned and then never really built. It sits out there now, north of Los Angeles, streets laid out but slowly being eaten by the sand and dirt. Still, 14,000 people lived there as of 2018. Design limitation or something stranger, this digital dead end is a reminder that all of this land, so modern, so ancient - all of this is spook country.

It's fitting I guess that GTA San Andreas is such a strange delight to return to. As I've waited, like everyone else, for GTA 6, I've been back here in the GTA games of the past, via the slightly dodgy remasters from a few years ago. And what's come through for me, spending a few idle mornings messing around in San Andreas, a game I thought I knew pretty well, is how beautifully odd everything is now. The best part of 20 years have given a game that once strained against its host technology a genuine aesthetic. Surprisingly sparse in detailing, as angular and sheer as dream architecture, in love with expanses, San Andreas is perfectly placed, it seems to me, to capture just how weird California can be.

California and Nevada. This is the part I always forget. I mainly think of San Andreas these days through the lens of GTA 5, which gives you LA and its surroundings but lets the single city serve as the star. It has a bigger footprint than the earlier game, sure, but it uses that space to go deep on the detailing and the sense of a singular, fiercely specific place. San Andreas meanwhile offers three cities - and one of them isn't actually in California. Alongside spins on LA and San Francisco, you get a version of Las Vegas. It's quite neat really: the three points of some arcane spiritual triangle. The kind of thing Marvel Whiteside Parsons, aerospace engineer and Actual Wizard, might have scratched in the dust of his backyard to help him control the weather. He lived - and died - in Pasadena, so all this would have been familiar anyway.

There is a classic moment in every GTA. A moment where the map opens up in the early games, or when you explore enough in the later games, and simply get to understand the audacity of what is being attempted here. In San Andreas, and this feels apt, the best way to have this moment is to break the rules, sneaking into the open map before the game is fully ready for you. An early mission allows you to step away from the critical path, as it were, and peg it to the airport in Los Santos, where a plane is waiting to be pinched. Taxi, feel the thrust, and you're off. LA beneath you - sorry, Los Santos - and on the horizon a dinkytown version of the Golden Gate Bridge and the MGM Grand.

Dinkytown is the word. From the sky, the huge map of San Andreas, which still feels like an incredible piece of work - such ambition! - can look like toys laid out on a duvet in the remasters. Coming back from GTA 5, a masterpiece of texture, the cities of San Andreas in particular feel very weird - cramped and condensed and wonderfully stagey, arranged the way a kid might jury-rig them together from shoeboxes and stray cardboard. On your way out of Grove Street early on you pass the Los Santos equivalent of the Hollywood sign, looming so close the way it does in the movies but not in the actual city and you feel you could touch it. Downtown is reduced to a handful of the best buildings - it's always pleasing to see the one from the brilliant first act of Speed made the cut - but still manages to capture the important stuff too, which in this case is the way that modern LA looms somewhat cruelly over the old city, captured with a strange sepulchral dignity in the form of the City Hall, so easy to spot but now so small!

Elsewhere, the ersatz Las Vega Strip looks like a drawing based on memory - a few of the classic shapes are there, but it's a sketch of a place. In this way, I guess, it captures a truth about Las Vegas. Also, perhaps, it's an admission that this is a town comfortably out of the reach of satire. As for San Francisco, it's a 60s cop movie delight to fishtail around. It nails the hills and what more do you need for a game with cars in it?

Taken as a whole these cities feel mesmerising now. And it's the limitations that make them sing, I think: those sheer granite canyons of downtown LA with its towering blocks of architecture and surprisingly thin traffic, with only the very faintest of ambient soundtracks that ensures any gunfire will pop and clap and shake you out of your seat. (Remember, I am playing the remasters and sound effects may be missing.) This is the LA of Repo Man rather than Heat - an otherland where the buildings feel alien, even the air feels alien. At night, palm trees stand out against a sky that glows a deep, clean red. GTA 5 would finesse all of this, the skyboxes and whatnot, but perhaps in that finessing something is lost. (Both games, incidentally, have such a talent for capturing California's kitsch excess that they perhaps miss some of the sweeter truths, such as the astonishing diversity of cultures a city like Los Angeles finds homes for. But how could any game do that justice?)

Dinkytowns aside, what San Andreas does better than GTA 5 is space - the peculiar flat spaces of Southern California in particular, a state in which you can never quite tell where the wilderness ends and the landscaping begins, and where the ancient rocks that announce the horizon can look new, like stage settings, while the freeways feel truly ancient, concrete lizards sleeping on the dirt, bleached by the easy sun. I can lose hours out here. I have had to double- and triple-check that GTA 5 is the bigger game, in fact, because San Andreas still feels absolutely enormous, a trick that works even when you can see the not-Golden Gate Bridge seconds after taking off from not-LAX. Grab a bike and you can spend an absolute age exploring the between-spaces which video game California and real California are both so good at conjuring, stumbling on little towns you will never stumble on again, passing commercial follies, listening as the radio pumps out "All My Exes Live in Texas".

And, paradoxically, the more I play the more I realise San Andreas is very good at the little things in its own odd way. GTA 5 is an astonishing feat of seeing and transposing, but I don't think it has anything across its acres to match the sense of place offered by San Andreas' take on Grove Street, where the story begins. It's just a neighborhood, but as you cross its small space and recross it in the game's opening hours, learning how to eat food and buy clothes and get a haircut and spray paint on walls and all that jazz, the sense that this is your home is grooved and regrooved into the experience. Maybe this is why it's such a thrill to return to this earlier pass at Los Santos. Because this one is where you truly live.

PC, PS3, PS4, PS5, Xbox 360, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Android, Mac, PC, PS2, PS3, PS4, Windows Phone, Xbox, Xbox 360, iOS

#10609. Alan Turing's biggest AI assumption may have been wrong

Alan Turing's famous ideas about artificial intelligence may have sent AI research down the wrong path for the past 75 years, according to prominent computer scientist Peter J. Denning.

In his new book, Turing's Mistake: Escaping the Yoke of Unintelligent Machines, Denning argues that two foundational assumptions made by Turing in 1950 continue to shape AI research today. The first is that intelligence can exist independently of a physical body and therefore be recreated in computer software. The second is that a machine can demonstrate intelligence by successfully imitating a human in conversation, an idea that later became known as the Turing test.

"These two claims have shaped much of AI research and development," Denning writes. "My premise is that our acquiescence to these claims has led to the AI mess in which we find ourselves today."

Denning argues that pursuing artificial general intelligence (AGI), or machines with human level intelligence, is unlikely to succeed. Instead, he warns, the technologies society is building could introduce significant new risks.

At the heart of Denning's argument is the idea of tacit knowledge, the vast amount of human understanding that cannot easily be put into words or represented in a form that computers can process.

He says machine learning cannot capture five major categories of tacit knowledge: common sense, everyday interactions with people and the environment, emotions and perception, practical performance skills, and the social and historical knowledge embedded in culture.

Researchers have long attempted to organize common sense into databases. One of the best known efforts was Douglas Lenat's Cyc project, which began in the 1980s with the goal of creating an extensive collection of common sense facts. After four decades of work, the project contained roughly 25 million entries.

"Yet even this treasury could not add up to a background of common sense sufficient to make expert systems smart enough to be experts," Denning notes. "Cyc validated that much of the knowledge that makes people experts cannot be articulated as propositions."

Denning believes practical skills present an even greater challenge.

"Our performance skills in thousands of domains cannot be communicated to machines," he explains. "Whereas descriptions of skillful outcomes ('know what') can often be represented as bits and stored in a machine, we do not know how to encode the embodied knowledge for skillful performance ('know how')."

He points to accomplished musicians as an example.

"A virtuoso violinist can play beautiful music yet cannot describe to an acolyte how to produce it.

"Even if a robot could observe and imitate skilled humans, having no biological body, a robot cannot grasp how the musician feels when playing beautiful music or how an audience feels when hearing it."

Denning also includes intuition, gut feelings, imagination, and spontaneous creativity among the forms of tacit knowledge that remain beyond the reach of machines.

Denning argues that all of these limitations stem from what he calls the "representation problem."

Computers can only perform calculations using data and instructions that have been encoded into physical forms they can recognize and process. Tacit knowledge, however, does not naturally fit into that framework.

"Behind every word is a deep well of tacit knowledge that gives it meaning," Denning says. "Words are but symbolic representations of meanings, not the meanings themselves. Commonly used Large Language Models, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini only manipulate words, they cannot know or understand the meaning of what they are saying."

According to Denning, this creates a fundamental divide. Because scientists still cannot fully explain how tacit knowledge works in humans, they also cannot translate it into a form machines can use.

"How we host tacit knowledge is largely a mystery," Denning admits. "All we know is that it is embodied. We have no idea what we might observe and measure in our bodies to reveal it."

Denning also argues that intelligence depends heavily on context, the surrounding circumstances that give words, actions, and decisions their meaning.

Context allows people to recognize sarcasm, humor, sincerity, and emotion. It helps determine when to be diplomatic, when to joke, and how to interpret countless social cues.

"When you inquire into where an assumption of the current context came from, you discover it rests on previous conversations from previous contexts. Each of those in turn rests on further previous conversations and their contexts. This pattern is endless and fractal," Denning explains.

Culture presents another major obstacle for AI.

Denning describes culture as encompassing values, norms, judgments, history, communities, moods, and even relationships involving power and care.

"Human conversations are imbued with background assumptions that give meaning and relevance to the words being used," Denning explains.

"Scaling up LLMs with ever larger neural networks will not enable them to acquire the embodied human knowledge we call culture. LLMs will not attain the objective of the Turing test: to demonstrate machine thought indistinguishable from human thought."

AI Safety and the Limits of Human Understanding

Denning concludes that humans and AI systems may ultimately develop different forms of tacit knowledge that neither can fully understand.

"Machines cannot read our tacit knowledge and we cannot read theirs," he writes. "We are aliens across an uncrossable divide."

He argues this gap raises serious concerns about AI safety. If machines cannot interpret the unspoken context behind human intentions, reliably aligning advanced AI systems with human goals may prove impossible.

"Through AI automation, agentic networks of machines are likely to develop their own machine intelligence that does not reach the level of human general intelligence but is still quite capable of creating severe problems for humans. This threat is a greater than a take-over by superintelligent machines," he explains.

"Machine intelligence has different concerns from us and does not appear to care about us. Its ways of thinking and problem-solving look alien to us. We do not yet know how to live safely with these machines.

"Pulling back from an AI automation singularity will demand much from us. We start by accepting that the familiar culture is fading away as intelligent machines appear in our society and we do not know what is coming. We decline to think like machines or be subservient to machines. We refuse to submit to a yoke imposed by low-intelligence machines. Most importantly, we reassert our humanity, declare once again what makes us different from machines, and celebrate those differences."

Materials provided by Taylor & Francis Group. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
